*Caption: the causal stem set out as a claim needing a mechanism and a defeater; the four optimist/critical pairs showing that the same capacity yields opposite outcomes depending on control, access and purpose; the four conversion conditions; the concession that technology reshapes which moral problems arise; and the link back to the non-inevitability argument of Session 12.*

#### In plain words

The 2019 owned part asks whether technological development leads to progress in the ethical standards of society. Notice the form: it is a **causal** question. That means two things are required and most scripts supply neither — a **mechanism** by which the alleged effect would occur, and a **defeater** that would break the mechanism.

Set out the optimist mechanism honestly. Communication technology puts people in contact with lives they would never otherwise encounter, and contact can widen sympathy. Medicine removes suffering that was previously simply endured. Productivity removes drudgery that consumed lives. Transparency tools make it harder to hide what is being done. Each of those is a real mechanism, not a straw position.

Now the defeater, and it is structural rather than anecdotal. Every one of those capacities is **double-edged in the same respect**. The same communication capacity that widens sympathy enables surveillance and manipulation. The same medicine that reduces suffering can be distributed unequally, so that the capacity exists and the relief does not. The same automation that removes drudgery displaces workers and strips skill out of work. The same information systems that support accountability encode and reproduce the bias in the data they were built from. ⚠️ These are not four separate complaints. They are one point made four times: **the direction of the effect is fixed by who controls the capacity, who can reach it, and what it is aimed at** — and none of those is supplied by the technology.

Hence the verdict: technical progress becomes social progress only when governed by ethical purposes, fair access, accountability and human control. And then the concession that lifts an answer above the average: the relation is not simply null. Technology **reshapes which moral problems exist**. It creates occasions for choice, harms that were not previously possible and duties that were not previously meaningful. What it does not do is supply the criterion by which any of them is settled.

#### The doctrine, exactly

✅ **The core proposition.** Technology changes capacities and social relations; it does not supply its own ends.

✅ **The optimist case.** Communication can widen knowledge and sympathy; medicine reduces suffering; productivity can reduce drudgery; transparency tools can aid accountability.

✅ **The critical case.** Surveillance and manipulation can intensify domination; access can remain unequal; automation can displace and deskill; information systems can reproduce bias.

⚠️ **The verdict.** Technical progress becomes social progress **only when governed by ethical purposes, fair access, accountability and human control**.

⚠️ **The concession that must be made.** The relation is not simply null: technology **does reshape the moral problems that arise**, creating new occasions for choice, new possible harms and new meaningful duties. Saying so pre-empts the objection that the "technology is neutral" position is too convenient.

⚠️ **Why this is Session 12 applied.** The non-inevitability argument runs: capacity enlarges for care and destruction alike; institutions distribute control and risk; moral learning can be reversed; therefore progress requires agency, institutions and judgment. The technology stem is that argument with one instance substituted, which is why the two sessions must be answered together.

⚠️ **The structural discipline for the 2019 answer.** ❌ Do **not** name a technology, platform, company, programme or episode. The clause is answered at the level of **capacity, control, access and purpose**, and an answer that reaches for named examples both breaches this package's evidence discipline and weakens the argument, because a named case invites a counter-case and the structural point does not.

#### Named evidence you can cite

- **The four optimist/critical pairs** ✅ — communication, medicine, productivity, transparency; each pair shows one capacity yielding opposite outcomes under different control, access and purpose.
- **The four conversion conditions** ✅ — ethical purposes, fair access, accountability, human control; the governing clause of the verdict and the sentence that converts a balanced description into a judgment.
- **The reshaping concession** ⚠️ — technology alters which moral problems arise without supplying the criterion for settling them; the refinement that distinguishes a controlled answer from the flat neutrality thesis.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Technology expands the range of what can be done and thereby enlarges the domain of moral choice; it supplies no criterion for choosing, so ethical progress depends on institutions and values rather than on technical capacity.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**capacity without ends** · **double-edged in the same respect** · **who controls, who can access, to what end** · **surveillance and manipulation** · **unequal access** · **displacement and deskilling** · **reproduced bias** · **ethical purposes, fair access, accountability, human control** · **reshapes the moral problems** · **enlarges the domain of choice**

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Technological development does not by itself produce ethical progress, because technology changes capacities and social relations without supplying the ends that would determine how those capacities are used. **Named evidence.** The point is established by the symmetry of the standard cases rather than by anecdote: communication can widen knowledge and sympathy while enabling surveillance and manipulation; medicine reduces suffering while access to it can remain unequal; productivity can reduce drudgery while automation displaces and deskills; and transparency tools can aid accountability while information systems reproduce the bias embedded in their inputs. **Analysis.** The four pairs are one argument made four times — the direction of the effect is fixed by who controls the capacity, who can reach it and what it is directed toward — so technical progress becomes social progress only where it is governed by ethical purposes, fair access, accountability and human control, and this is simply the non-inevitability argument applied: capacity enlarges for care and destruction alike, institutions distribute its control and risks, and moral learning can be reversed. **Qualification.** The relation is nevertheless not null, and an answer that treats technology as inert has overstated its case: technology reshapes which moral problems arise at all, creating occasions for choice, harms that were not previously possible and duties that were not previously meaningful — what it does not do is supply the criterion by which any of those is settled.

#### Objection → reply → residual limit

- **Objection.** ✅ The neutrality thesis is too convenient. Some technologies are not merely usable for good or ill but carry a **built-in tendency** — a capacity that requires centralised control to operate at all will produce centralisation whoever owns it, so the ends are not wholly external to the artefact.
- **Reply.** ⚠️ The objection is largely correct and should be absorbed rather than resisted, because absorbing it strengthens the position. The claim being defended is not that technologies are inert but that they are **not self-justifying**: where an artefact has a built-in tendency, that tendency is itself a proper object of ethical and political assessment before adoption — which is precisely the point that the four conversion conditions make, since "human control" is meaningless if the artefact's operation forecloses it.
- **Residual limit.** ❓ Assessment before adoption is far easier to state than to perform, since the social effects of a capacity are frequently unknown until it is widely deployed, and by then the arrangements built around it are costly to reverse. The precautionary logic used in the ecological sessions applies here too, and so does its cost: restraint under uncertainty forgoes benefits that may have been real.

#### Examiner traps on this session

1. Assuming that technology creates ethical progress. The stem is a causal claim and the assumption is exactly what it is testing.
2. Producing a "balanced view" — some benefits, some harms — with no criterion. The pairs are evidence for a structural claim, not a two-column list to be left unresolved.
3. Naming technologies, platforms, companies or episodes. ❌ Prohibited by this package's evidence discipline, and analytically weaker than the structural argument.
4. Denying any relation between technology and morality. The reshaping concession must be made; the flat neutrality thesis is easy to refute and reads as evasion.

#### Mini recap

The stem is causal, so supply a mechanism and a defeater. Technology changes capacities and relations; it does not supply ends. Four optimist mechanisms — sympathy, relief of suffering, reduced drudgery, accountability — each matched by a critical counterpart under different control, access or purpose. Conversion conditions: ethical purposes, fair access, accountability, human control. Concede that technology reshapes which moral problems arise without settling them. It is the non-inevitability argument applied. Name no technology, platform, company or episode.

```text
  CLOSURE FLOW -- SESSION 16
  "does technology bring ethical progress?" -> a CAUSAL claim
      -> MECHANISM: capacity changes; ends are not supplied by the artefact
      -> CONSEQUENCE: direction fixed by CONTROL | ACCESS | PURPOSE; four
         conversion conditions required
      -> TRAP: assumed progress; unresolved balance; named cases; flat
         neutrality
      -> ANSWER USE: mechanism, defeater, conversion conditions, then the
         reshaping concession as the closing refinement
```

```text
   +---+------------------------+------------------------------------+
   | 4 | RAPID TECHNOLOGICAL    | a skill taught as a terminal       |
   |   | CHANGE causes          | qualification can expire; the      |
   |   | OBSOLESCENCE           | capability must include the        |
   |   |                        | capacity to RE-LEARN               |
   +---+------------------------+------------------------------------+

   THE TWO ERRORS TO AVOID -- THEY ARE MIRROR IMAGES
   +----------------------------+----------------------------------+
   | "SKILL EDUCATION IS        | "SKILL EDUCATION IS MERELY       |
   |  SUFFICIENT"               |  INSTRUMENTAL"                   |
   | -- defeated by limit 1:    | -- defeated by DIGNITY OF        |
   |    capability without an   |    LABOUR: competence and self-   |
   |    outlet is frustrated,   |    respect are part of a          |
   |    not enlarged            |    developed life, not only a     |
   |                            |    means to income                |
   +----------------------------+----------------------------------+
             |                                   |
             +----------------+------------------+
                              v
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | THE POSITION: SKILL EDUCATION IS NEITHER SUFFICIENT NOR MERELY     |
   | INSTRUMENTAL. IT SHOULD COMBINE LIVELIHOOD, ADAPTABILITY, DIGNITY  |
   | OF LABOUR AND CRITICAL CITIZENSHIP.                                |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+

   THE AGENCY TEST -- WHY THIS SESSION NEEDS SESSION 15
     Does the skill enlarge the learner's BARGAINING POWER, or only her
     USEFULNESS TO AN EMPLOYER? If only the second, the person has been
     made a more productive INSTRUMENT and not a freer AGENT -- which is
     development counted in the wrong space.
```

*Caption: the four-step positive argument stated at its strongest, the four limits each framed as a paragraph, the two mirror-image errors with the argument that defeats each, the position that combines livelihood with adaptability, dignity and citizenship, and the agency test that connects the session to the empowerment ladder.*

#### In plain words

The 2023 owned part asks whether emphasis on skill education will enhance development. It is an **Evaluate** stem, which means a standard has to be named and applied — and the standard, in this owner, is capability rather than employment.

Take the positive case seriously first. The capability approach insists that formal opportunity is not enough: a right to work that a person cannot exercise, or schooling that does not convert into anything she can do, expands nothing. Skills are exactly the kind of thing that improves **conversion** — they turn education into work, work into income, income into options, and options into the ability to bargain rather than to accept. Productive participation also enlarges what a person contributes, and contribution is connected to standing. So the positive argument is strong, and it is a *capability* argument rather than an employment argument.

⚠️ Then the limits, and each one is a paragraph rather than a phrase. First: a skill without decent work available produces **frustrated capability** — the capacity is created and then blocked, which is worse than not creating it, because the person now knows what she could be doing. Second: if training is narrow, it displaces critical, civic and humanistic education, and the person is equipped as a worker while being disequipped as a citizen. Third: access to the high-return tracks is itself structured by inherited advantage, so a skill programme can reproduce the hierarchy it was meant to relax. Fourth: technology changes, and a skill taught as a terminal qualification expires — which means the real capability is the capacity to **re-learn**, not the particular competence.

The two errors are mirror images. Saying skill education is sufficient ignores limit 1. Saying it is merely instrumental ignores the **dignity of labour** — competence and self-respect are part of a developed life, not only a route to income. The examinable position holds both ends: livelihood, adaptability, dignity of labour and critical citizenship together.

#### The doctrine, exactly

✅ **The positive claim.** Skill education can expand capability by improving competence, work options, productivity and self-respect.

✅ **The argument.** (1) Capability requires effective means, not formal opportunity alone. (2) Relevant skills improve the conversion of education into work and agency. (3) Productive participation can enlarge social contribution and bargaining power. (4) Therefore skill education can enhance development.

✅ **The four limits.** Skills without decent work create **frustrated capability**; narrow employability can **displace critical, civic and humanistic education**; unequal access **reproduces hierarchy**; rapidly changing technology can make training **obsolete**.

⚠️ **The position.** Skill education is **neither sufficient nor merely instrumental**: it should combine **livelihood, adaptability, dignity of labour and critical citizenship**.

⚠️ **The agency test.** Ask whether the skill enlarges the learner's **bargaining power** or only her usefulness to an employer. If only the second, the person has been made a more productive instrument rather than a freer agent — development counted in the wrong informational space.

⚠️ **The evaluative discipline for an "Evaluate" stem.** Name the standard (capability and agency), apply it, concede the counter-case (the limits), and deliver a graded verdict. ❌ Do **not** answer with employment figures, programme names or outlays; that is an Economy answer and this owner supplies no data.

#### Named evidence you can cite

- **The four-step capability argument for skills** ✅ — effective means, conversion, contribution and bargaining power; the way to make the positive case a *philosophical* case rather than an employment case.
- **Frustrated capability** ⚠️ — the reconstruction that names the sharpest limit: a created capability with no outlet, which is a distinctive harm and not merely an absence of benefit.
- **Dignity of labour** ✅ — the Gandhian criterion that defeats the "merely instrumental" reading, and the bridge into Session 18.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> Skill education enhances development only where it enlarges the learner's bargaining power rather than merely her usefulness to an employer: competence without decent work is a capability created and then blocked, and employability purchased by displacing civic and critical education equips a worker while disequipping a citizen.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**capability requires effective means** · **conversion of education into work** · **bargaining power** · **frustrated capability** · **narrow employability** · **critical, civic and humanistic education** · **unequal access reproduces hierarchy** · **obsolescence and the capacity to re-learn** · **dignity of labour** · **neither sufficient nor merely instrumental**

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** Emphasis on skill education enhances development conditionally rather than automatically, and the condition is that it enlarge capability and agency rather than employability alone. **Named evidence.** The positive case is a capability case: since capability requires effective means and not formal opportunity alone, relevant skills improve the conversion of education into work and of work into agency, and productive participation enlarges both social contribution and bargaining power. **Analysis.** Four limits determine whether the conversion actually occurs — skills without decent work produce frustrated capability, in which a capacity is created and then blocked; narrow employability displaces the critical, civic and humanistic education that equips a citizen rather than only a worker; unequal access to the high-return tracks reproduces the hierarchy the programme was meant to relax; and rapid technological change makes a terminal qualification expire, so the durable capability is the capacity to re-learn. **Qualification.** The verdict must therefore avoid two mirror-image errors: skill education is not sufficient, because a capability without an outlet is frustrated rather than enlarged, and it is not merely instrumental, because competence and self-respect belong to a developed life and not only to a means of income — the defensible position combines livelihood, adaptability, dignity of labour and critical citizenship, and the operative test is whether the learner's bargaining power has grown.

#### Objection → reply → residual limit

- **Objection.** ✅ The demand that skill education also carry critical and civic education is a luxury argument. For a person without work, employability now is worth more than citizenship capacities later, and insisting on the broader curriculum delays the relief that is actually needed.
- **Reply.** ⚠️ The objection assumes the two are substitutes and they are not, for a reason internal to the capability framework. A narrowly trained worker whose occupation is displaced has lost the whole of her acquired capability; a worker whose training included general reasoning, literacy and the capacity to re-learn retains the means of acquiring another. Adaptability is therefore not an ornament attached to skilling but the part of it that survives — and the civic capacities are what allow the worker to contest the terms on which her work is organised rather than only to accept them.
- **Residual limit.** ❓ The reply is comparative and does not settle the **allocation** question in conditions of severe scarcity: where resources permit only one, an argument for immediate employability is not irrational, and the capability framework by itself does not fix the discount rate between present relief and durable capacity. Naming that indeterminacy is more honest than resolving it by assertion.

#### Examiner traps on this session

1. Reducing skill education to employment outcomes. ❌ Employment statistics, scheme names and outlays are outside this owner and cannot be sourced within it.
2. Answering "yes, skills are important". The stem is Evaluate; a standard must be named and applied and a graded verdict delivered.
3. Omitting frustrated capability. It is the sharpest of the four limits and it distinguishes a philosophical answer from an administrative one.
4. Treating skill purely instrumentally. Dignity of labour and self-respect are part of the developed life, and the Gandhian criterion is available here without importing the whole doctrine.

#### Mini recap

Conditional verdict, conditions carry the marks. Positive argument: capability needs effective means; skills improve conversion; participation enlarges contribution and bargaining power. Four limits: frustrated capability without decent work; narrow employability displacing civic and critical education; unequal access reproducing hierarchy; obsolescence, which makes the capacity to re-learn the durable capability. Two mirror errors: sufficiency, and mere instrumentality. Position: livelihood, adaptability, dignity of labour and critical citizenship together. Test: bargaining power, not usefulness to an employer.

```text
  CLOSURE FLOW -- SESSION 17
  skill education -> a CAPABILITY argument, not an employment argument
      -> MECHANISM: skills improve CONVERSION of education into work and
         agency, and can raise BARGAINING POWER
      -> CONSEQUENCE: four limits decide the case -- frustrated capability,
         narrowness, unequal access, obsolescence
      -> TRAP: employment data; "yes, skills matter"; omitting frustrated
         capability; purely instrumental reading
      -> ANSWER USE: name the standard, apply it, concede the limits, close
         with livelihood + adaptability + dignity + citizenship
```

### SESSION 18 — GANDHIAN SOCIAL DEVELOPMENT AND THE AMBEDKARITE SAFEGUARD

#### The picture first

```text
   THE ARGUMENT -- FIVE STEPS. STATE THEM AS AN ARGUMENT, NOT AS IDEALS.

  |                    | AMBEDKAR'S CRITICISM IS **INDISPENSABLE**,   |
  |                    | NOT OPTIONAL.                                |
  +--------------------+----------------------------------------------+
  | TECHNOLOGICAL      | small-scale production cannot meet complex   |
  | CONSERVATISM       | modern needs                                 |
  |                    | REPLY: read Gandhianism as a TEST OF SCALE,  |
  |                    | CONTROL AND NEED -- not a ban on every       |
  |                    | machine                                      |
  +--------------------+----------------------------------------------+
  | TRUSTEESHIP IS     | owners retain power; obligation is voluntary |
  | VOLUNTARY          | REPLY: trusteeship MORALLY DELEGITIMISES     |

  |                    | MECHANISMS are needed to convert obligation  |
  |                    | into ACCOUNTABILITY                          |
  +--------------------+----------------------------------------------+

   GANDHI vs INDUSTRIAL MODERNISATION -- THE AXIS OF DISAGREEMENT
     MODERNISATION values SCALE, PRODUCTIVITY, MASTERY OVER SCARCITY
     GANDHI asks whether SCALE CENTRALISES POWER, MULTIPLIES WANTS,
       DEGRADES LABOUR OR NATURE

             SELF-RULE (swaraj)
     AMBEDKAR: the village can INSTITUTIONALISE CASTE EXCLUSION
     VERDICT: local self-government is progressive ONLY WHERE
       CONSTITUTIONAL RIGHTS, MOBILITY AND ANTI-CASTE SAFEGUARDS
       TRANSFORM LOCAL POWER.

      Ambedkar debate belong to CASTE DISCRIMINATION and are ROUTED.)
```

*Caption: the Gandhian conception as a structured argument rather than a set of ideals — five steps, four presuppositions, three objections with their replies — followed by the two comparisons that decide the answer: against industrial modernisation, and against Ambedkar on the village.*

#### In plain words

The 2020 owned part asks the candidate to **state and examine** the Gandhian concept of social development. "State and examine" is a two-part instruction and both parts are scored: an exposition that never turns critical, and a critique that never states the position accurately, each lose half the marks.

The exposition has to present Gandhi as making an **argument**, not as expressing preferences. The argument begins with a psychological and social claim: wants that are not limited generate dependence, competition and eventually violence, because unlimited demand cannot be satisfied and must be fought over. It continues with a structural claim: when production is centralised and large, it becomes detached from what people actually need and from any control they could exercise over it. It adds the moral claim that gives Gandhi his distinctive shape: **means shape ends**, so a development achieved by violence or degradation cannot deliver a non-violent, dignified society at the end. And it concludes that if self-rule is to be real, the economic and moral capacities that support it must exist locally — from which decentralised, need-oriented, ecologically restrained development follows.

⚠️ Then the examination. Three objections, each with a reply, and the first of them is compulsory in any Indian answer. **Villages can reproduce caste and patriarchy**, and a scheme of decentralisation that ignores this hands power to precisely the local hierarchies it romanticises. Ambedkar's criticism is therefore **indispensable**, and an answer that omits it will be read as uncritical. The reply is not to abandon decentralisation but to condition it: it is defensible only with equal rights and anti-domination guarantees. The second objection — that small-scale production cannot meet complex needs — is answered by reading Gandhianism as a **test of scale, control and need** rather than a prohibition on machines. The third — that trusteeship leaves owners in power — is answered by conceding the point about enforcement while insisting that trusteeship morally delegitimises absolute ownership, and that **institutional mechanisms** are required to convert obligation into accountability.

#### The doctrine, exactly

✅ **Doctrine statement.** Gandhian development seeks the welfare and moral elevation of all (*sarvodaya*) through self-rule (*swarāj*), non-violence, decentralisation, dignity of labour, trusteeship, restraint of wants and village-centred production.

✅ **The five-step argument.** (1) Unlimited wants create dependence, competition and violence. (2) Centralised industrial power separates production from human need and local control. (3) Means shape ends, so violent or degrading development cannot yield a non-violent good society. (4) Self-rule requires economic and moral capacities at the local level. (5) Therefore development must be decentralised, need-oriented and ecologically restrained.

✅ **The four presuppositions.** Moral self-limitation is politically relevant; small-scale association can sustain participation; work has ethical and community value beyond income; social change can proceed through non-violent conversion and constructive action.

✅ **Objection one — the romantic village.** Villages may reproduce caste and patriarchy. ⚠️ **Reply:** decentralisation is defensible only with equal rights and anti-domination; **Ambedkar's criticism is indispensable**.

✅ **Objection two — technological conservatism.** Small-scale production cannot meet complex modern needs. ⚠️ **Reply:** Gandhianism can be read as a **test of scale, control and need**, not a ban on every machine.

✅ **Objection three — trusteeship as voluntary paternalism.** Owners retain power. ✅ **Reply:** trusteeship **morally delegitimises absolute ownership**, but institutional mechanisms are needed to convert obligation into accountability.

✅ **Gandhi against industrial modernisation.** Industrial modernisation values scale, productivity and mastery over scarcity; Gandhi asks whether scale centralises power, multiplies wants and degrades labour or nature. ⚠️ A defensible synthesis adopts technology **selectively** according to capability, decentralised control, ecological cost and non-domination.

✅ **Gandhi against Ambedkar on the village.** Gandhi treats the self-governing village as a potential school of self-rule (*swarāj*); Ambedkar warns that the village can institutionalise caste exclusion. ⚠️ Local self-government is progressive **only where constitutional rights, mobility and anti-caste safeguards transform local power**. ⚠️ This is a bounded bridge: the full caste doctrine and the wider debate belong to **Caste Discrimination: Gandhi and Ambedkar** and are routed.

#### Named evidence you can cite

- **Gandhi, *Hind Swaraj*** and his writings on trusteeship, self-rule (*swarāj*) and welfare of all (*sarvodaya*) ✅ — cited by author and title only, with no page, edition or wording asserted.
- **The means-shape-ends principle** ✅ — the claim that gives the Gandhian argument its distinctive structure and prevents it from being read as mere preference for smallness.
- **Ambedkar's warning about the village** ✅ — that local self-government can institutionalise caste exclusion; the compulsory counterweight in any Gandhian development answer, used here as a bounded criterion.
- **The selective-adoption synthesis** ⚠️ — technology chosen by capability, decentralised control, ecological cost and non-domination; the reconstruction that answers the anti-modern charge without abandoning the critique.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The Gandhian conception is best read not as nostalgia for the village but as a test applied to any development proposal — does this scale centralise power, does it multiply wants, does it degrade labour or nature, and could those who bear its consequences govern it? — and it is defensible only when joined to Ambedkar's insistence that a village which preserves caste power is not self-rule.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**welfare of all (sarvodaya)** · **self-rule (swarāj)** · **trusteeship** · **restraint of wants** · **dignity of labour** · **decentralisation** · **means shape ends** · **test of scale, control and need** · **moral delegitimation of absolute ownership** · **anti-caste safeguards** · **school of self-rule against institutionalised exclusion**

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** The Gandhian conception of social development is an argument rather than a set of preferences, and its conclusion — that development must be decentralised, need-oriented and ecologically restrained — follows from premises that can be stated and tested. **Named evidence.** In *Hind Swaraj* and in the writings on trusteeship, self-rule (*swarāj*) and welfare of all (*sarvodaya*), the argument runs that unlimited wants generate dependence, competition and violence; that centralised industrial power separates production from human need and from local control; that means shape ends, so a development achieved through violence or degradation cannot produce a non-violent society; and that self-rule requires economic and moral capacities at the local level. **Analysis.** Read this way the conception functions as a test applied to any proposal — whether the scale centralises power, whether wants are multiplied, whether labour or nature is degraded, and whether those who bear the consequences can govern it — which is why the charge of technological conservatism misses: the position discriminates by scale, control and need rather than prohibiting machinery, and trusteeship morally delegitimises absolute ownership even though institutional mechanisms are required to convert that obligation into accountability. **Qualification.** The decisive objection is Ambedkar's, and it is indispensable rather than optional: a village may institutionalise caste exclusion, so decentralisation is progressive only where constitutional rights, mobility and anti-caste safeguards transform local power — which means the Gandhian conception survives examination in a conditioned form, as a test of scale and control operating inside guarantees it does not itself supply.

#### Objection → reply → residual limit

- **Objection.** ✅ Restraint of wants is a demand that falls unequally. Asking for self-limitation from people who have very little is not asceticism but acquiescence in their deprivation, and a doctrine that praises simplicity risks moralising poverty into a virtue.
- **Reply.** ⚠️ The restraint claim is directed at **multiplied and manufactured wants** rather than at need, and its target is the dynamic in which satisfaction generates further demand — a dynamic that operates at the top of a distribution rather than at its bottom. Read that way it is structurally the same asymmetry that the ecological argument requires: restraint where consumption is far above what any defensible account of need would support, and expansion where it is far below.
- **Residual limit.** ❓ Gandhi's own account does not settle the boundary between need and multiplied want, and any specification of that boundary is contestable and can be used coercively against the poor rather than the affluent. The doctrine supplies a direction and not a line, and an answer that pretends otherwise has attributed more precision to it than it possesses.

#### Examiner traps on this session

1. Presenting Gandhi as nostalgic preference for village life. It is an argument with stated premises, and it must be reconstructed as one.
2. Omitting Ambedkar. ⚠️ In an Indian answer on Gandhian development this is the single most damaging omission, and the criticism is indispensable rather than optional.
3. Expanding into the full Gandhi–Ambedkar caste debate. That belongs to **Caste Discrimination: Gandhi and Ambedkar**; here the objection functions as a bounded criterion.
4. Treating trusteeship as an institutional solution. It is a moral delegitimation of absolute ownership; the enforcement gap must be conceded and answered with institutional mechanisms.
5. Answering "state and examine" with exposition alone, or with criticism alone. Both halves are scored.

#### Mini recap

Doctrine: welfare of all (*sarvodaya*) through self-rule (*swarāj*), non-violence, decentralisation, dignity of labour, trusteeship, restraint of wants, village-centred production. Argument in five steps ending in decentralised, need-oriented, ecologically restrained development. Four presuppositions. Three objections: romantic village — answered only by equal rights and anti-domination, with Ambedkar indispensable; technological conservatism — answered by the test of scale, control and need; trusteeship as voluntary paternalism — answered by moral delegitimation plus institutional mechanisms. Two comparisons: against industrial modernisation, selective adoption; against Ambedkar, local self-government progressive only under constitutional rights, mobility and anti-caste safeguards.

```text
  CLOSURE FLOW -- SESSION 18
  Gandhian social development -> an ARGUMENT: unlimited wants -> centralised
      power -> means shape ends -> local capacity -> decentralised,
      need-oriented, restrained development
      -> MECHANISM: a TEST of scale, control and need applied to any proposal
      -> CONSEQUENCE: survives in a CONDITIONED form -- inside rights,
         mobility and anti-caste safeguards it does not itself supply
      -> TRAP: nostalgia reading; omitting Ambedkar; importing the full caste
         debate; trusteeship treated as enforcement; exposition without
         examination
      -> ANSWER USE: state the five steps, run the three objections, close on
         the conditioned verdict
```

### SESSION 19 — ECOLOGICAL POLITICAL PHILOSOPHY: STANDING, LIMITS AND FUTURE JUSTICE

#### The picture first

```text

   underneath it. The prior questions are:
     WHAT HAS MORAL STANDING? | IS PERMANENT GROWTH COHERENT? |
     WHAT IS OWED TO THE UNBORN? | WHO BEARS THE COST OF ADJUSTMENT?

   BLOCK A -- MORAL STANDING: THE THREE ORIENTATIONS
  +---------------+---------------------+--------------------------+
  | ORIENTATION   | WHO OR WHAT COUNTS  | CHARACTERISTIC WEAKNESS  |
  +---------------+---------------------+--------------------------+
  | ANTHROPO-     | HUMAN BEINGS ONLY.  | protects only what       |
  | CENTRIC       | Nature has          | humans happen to need or |
  |               | INSTRUMENTAL value  | value; permits          |
  |               | -- resource, life-  | destruction where NO     |
  |               | support, amenity,   | HUMAN INTEREST is        |
  |               | heritage for future | engaged                  |
  |               | humans              |                          |
  +---------------+---------------------+--------------------------+
  | BIOCENTRIC    | ALL INDIVIDUAL      | no principled way to     |
  |               | LIVING BEINGS, each | ADJUDICATE BETWEEN       |

  |               | ITS OWN             | protect a SPECIES or a   |
  |               |                     | SYSTEM as such           |
  +---------------+---------------------+--------------------------+
  | ECOCENTRIC    | ECOLOGICAL WHOLES   | risks SUBORDINATING      |
  |               | -- species,         | INDIVIDUALS, human and   |

  |               | ecosystems, the     | of the whole             |
  |               | biotic community    |                          |
  +---------------+---------------------+--------------------------+

   THE DISCRIMINATING QUESTION -- ONE LINE THAT PROVES THEY DIFFER
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | WOULD IT BE WRONG TO DESTROY A WILDERNESS THAT NO HUMAN BEING WILL |
   | EVER SEE, USE OR KNOW OF?                                          |
   |   ANTHROPOCENTRIC: NO.  BIOCENTRIC: YES (individual goods).         |
   |   ECOCENTRIC: YES (systemic integrity).                             |
   | THEY ARE DISTINCT POSITIONS, NOT DEGREES OF THE SAME CONCERN.       |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+

   THE PRACTICAL WARNING FOR AN EXAMINATION
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | DO NOT ADOPT STRONG ECOCENTRISM CASUALLY. PRESSED CONSISTENTLY IT  |
   | LICENSES COERCIVE LIMITS ON HUMAN BEINGS FOR SYSTEMIC GOODS (the   |
   | ECO-AUTHORITARIAN RISK IN BLOCK F) AND SITS BADLY WITH THE         |
   | CAPABILITY FRAMEWORK THIS OWNER RELIES ON. THE DEFENSIBLE          |
   | POSITION FOR A DEVELOPMENT ANSWER IS A STRONG, NON-INSTRUMENTAL    |
   | ANTHROPOCENTRISM SUPPLEMENTED BY DUTIES OF STEWARDSHIP -- STATED   |
   | AS A POSITION AND DEFENDED, NOT A SLOGAN OF "HARMONY WITH NATURE". |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+

   BLOCK B -- NAESS (1973): SHALLOW AND DEEP ECOLOGY
     SHALLOW: fights POLLUTION and RESOURCE DEPLETION, with the central
              objective being the HEALTH AND AFFLUENCE OF PEOPLE IN THE
              DEVELOPED COUNTRIES. Concern real, but DERIVATIVE.
     DEEP:    asks about the UNDERLYING ASSUMPTIONS instead of the
              symptoms. Affirms INTRINSIC VALUE of non-human life
              independent of usefulness; treats RICHNESS AND DIVERSITY
              of life forms as values in themselves; calls for a change
              in the IDEOLOGICAL FRAMEWORK -- in the human self-
              understanding that places humanity outside and above
              nature.
     THE DIFFERENCE IS ONE OF **DEPTH OF QUESTIONING**, NOT OF DEGREE
     OF CONCERN. Say this exactly; it is the examinable formulation.

     THE FIVE-STEP RECONSTRUCTION
       1. policy addressed to SYMPTOMS leaves the GENERATING FRAMEWORK
          intact
       2. that framework treats nature as a STANDING RESERVE and
          ever-rising consumption as the measure of a good life
       3. while it is intact, EFFICIENCY GAINS ARE ABSORBED by expanded
          consumption and problems reappear AT A LARGER SCALE
       4. therefore an adequate response revises the FRAMEWORK -- the
          conception of the self, of the good life, of humanity's place
       5. hence the difference is DEPTH OF QUESTIONING

   BLOCK C -- PRODUCTIVISM AND THE DEGROWTH ARGUMENT
     PRODUCTIVISM: the assumption -- shared across otherwise opposed
       ideologies -- that EXPANDING PRODUCTION is the MASTER OBJECTIVE
       of social organisation, both the measure of success and the
       solvent of distributive conflict.
     NOTE: THIS CRITIQUE APPLIES TO INDUSTRIAL SOCIALISM AS MUCH AS TO
       CAPITALISM. Making that point prevents the answer from collapsing
       into a generic anti-market answer.

     THE DEGROWTH ARGUMENT -- SIX STEPS
       1. economic activity is EMBEDDED in a biophysical system with
          FINITE SINKS AND STOCKS
       2. efficiency gains per unit of output have been OFFSET by
          expansion in the VOLUME of output -- relative decoupling has
          not delivered absolute reduction at the required scale
       3. beyond a threshold, further aggregate output adds little to
          health, longevity, education or reported well-being IN
          AFFLUENT SOCIETIES
       4. therefore PERMANENT AGGREGATE GROWTH is neither ecologically
          feasible nor developmentally necessary for the already-affluent
       5. hence the objective is PLANNED REDUCTION IN THROUGHPUT, with
          provisioning reorganised around SUFFICIENCY, CARE, SHORTER
          WORKING TIME and EQUALITY
       6. and -- CRITICALLY -- the burden falls on HIGH-CONSUMING
          economies and classes, **NOT** on societies below the
          deprivation threshold
     WEAKEST LINK (say so): that a NON-GROWING economy can maintain
       employment, public services and social stability.
     THE ASYMMETRY THAT MUST BE STATED: CONTRACTION AND CONVERGENCE --
       reduction far above a defensible threshold, EXPANSION far below.
       AN ANSWER THAT APPLIES DEGROWTH UNIFORMLY TO INDIA HAS
       MISUNDERSTOOD THE ARGUMENT.
     THE CAPABILITY BRIDGE: if development is expansion of CAPABILITY
       rather than of OUTPUT, then beyond the level at which output
       secures capability, further growth has NO AUTOMATIC
       DEVELOPMENTAL CLAIM. Sen's framework reaches the same conclusion
       by a NON-ECOLOGICAL route -- joining the two is a strong move.

   BLOCK D -- INTERGENERATIONAL JUSTICE, AND ITS THREE PROBLEMS
     THE CLAIM: present generations owe duties of JUSTICE, not merely of
       BENEVOLENCE, to those not yet born -- most plausibly a duty NOT
       TO FORECLOSE their capacity to meet their own needs and live
       lives they have reason to value. The BRUNDTLAND REPORT (1987)
       builds this into the concept of sustainable development itself.
       PARAPHRASE IT; NEVER REPRODUCE IT VERBATIM.
     PROBLEM 1 . THE NON-IDENTITY PROBLEM
       different policies produce DIFFERENT FUTURE PEOPLE. A person born
       into a degraded world owes her EXISTENCE to the policy that
       degraded it; if her life is worth living she cannot claim to have
       been made WORSE OFF BY THAT POLICY.
       RESPONSE: shift from PERSON-AFFECTING to IMPERSONAL principles --
       what matters is the QUALITY OF LIVES LIVED, whoever lives them --
       or ground the duty in the value of the RESOURCE BASE.
     PROBLEM 2 . MOTIVATION AND RECIPROCITY
       future people cannot reciprocate, vote, bargain or sanction, so
       MUTUAL-ADVANTAGE contractarianism struggles to generate the duty.
     PROBLEM 3 . DISCOUNTING
       standard appraisal DISCOUNTS distant costs, making catastrophic
       distant harm look trivial today. The objection: a PURE TIME
       PREFERENCE -- counting a person's interests for less MERELY
       BECAUSE SHE EXISTS LATER -- is arbitrary in the way that counting
       them for less because she lives FURTHER AWAY is arbitrary.
     THE SPECIFICATION THAT ANSWERS INDETERMINACY: the duty is to
       PRESERVE OPTIONS AND AVOID IRREVERSIBILITY -- protect the
       CAPACITY TO CHOOSE, not the CONTENT of the choice. Uncertainty is
       therefore an argument FOR PRECAUTION about irreversible losses,
       not for inaction.

   BLOCK E -- THE ENVIRONMENTALISM OF THE POOR (GUHA AND
   MARTINEZ-ALIER, *VARIETIES OF ENVIRONMENTALISM*, 1997)
     THE CLAIM: environmentalism in the global South is typically NOT a
       post-material concern of the affluent for wilderness and amenity,
       but a struggle over LIVELIHOOD -- access to forests, water,
       grazing, fisheries and clean air by communities whose subsistence
       depends DIRECTLY on them.
     THE FIVE-STEP ARGUMENT
       1. a dominant account treats environmental concern as emerging
          ONLY AFTER material needs are satisfied
       2. but many of the most sustained conflicts occur AMONG THE
          POOREST, over resources on which they DIRECTLY DEPEND
       3. these struggles are SIMULTANEOUSLY DISTRIBUTIONAL AND
          ECOLOGICAL -- who bears the ecological cost, who is displaced
       4. therefore environment and development are NOT OPPOSED
          OBJECTIVES AT ALL for such communities: the two are the SAME
          QUESTION
       5. hence "ENVIRONMENT VERSUS DEVELOPMENT" IS A FRAMING AVAILABLE
          ONLY TO THOSE WHOSE SUBSISTENCE IS NOT AT STAKE
     THE REFRAMING -- THE HIGHEST-VALUE MOVE IN THIS SESSION
       The standard stem presupposes that the AFFECTED PEOPLE ARE THE
       BENEFICIARIES. Where costs and benefits fall on DIFFERENT
       POPULATIONS the real question is DISTRIBUTIVE: WHO GAINS, WHO
       BEARS THE RISK, WHO DECIDES, AND ON WHAT TERMS.
     OBJECTION: it ROMANTICISES the poor as natural conservationists,
       when poverty also drives degradation.
     REPLY: the thesis is about the STRUCTURE OF CONFLICT, not
       ecological virtue. It claims the poor fight over resources they
       depend on -- compatible with degradation under INSECURE TENURE.
       The strongest version argues that SECURE RIGHTS convert
       DEPENDENCE INTO STEWARDSHIP.

   BLOCK F -- THE ECO-AUTHORITARIAN RISK AND THE JUST-TRANSITION REPLY
     THE ARGUMENT THE ANSWER MUST DEFEAT (state it fairly first)
       1. ecological limits are HARD CONSTRAINTS; breaching them is
          catastrophic and PARTLY IRREVERSIBLE
       2. democratic processes are SLOW, SHORT-HORIZONED and vulnerable
          to organised interests and to voters bearing costs now for
          benefits later
       3. therefore ecological survival MAY REQUIRE INSULATING decisions
          from democratic contestation
     FOUR REPLIES, IN ASCENDING STRENGTH
       1. EPISTEMIC ... ecological policy depends on DISPERSED LOCAL
          KNOWLEDGE of soils, water, seasons and species that
          centralised authority does not possess
       2. COMPLIANCE .. measures imposed without consent are EVADED,
          REVERSED at the next opportunity, and require enforcement
          capacity that itself consumes resources
       3. RECORD ...... insulation from accountability has NOT
          historically produced ecological restraint; suppressing
          information and dissent REMOVES THE FEEDBACK that detects
          damage early
       4. DISTRIBUTIVE -- THE DECISIVE ONE ... where the burden of
          adjustment falls on those LEAST ABLE TO BEAR IT, ecological
          policy will be neither JUST nor DURABLE. A JUST TRANSITION
          conditions ecological restructuring on PRIOR GUARANTEES of
          livelihood, retraining, compensation and participation.
     VERDICT TO STATE: the ecological case STRENGTHENS rather than
       weakens the argument for PARTICIPATION, because durable
       ecological transformation requires the CONSENT AND KNOWLEDGE of
       those whose livelihoods it reorganises.
```

*Caption: the ecological module in six blocks — the three orientations on moral standing with the discriminating wilderness question and the practical warning against casual ecocentrism; Naess's depth-of-questioning distinction; productivism and the six-step degrowth argument with its asymmetry and capability bridge; intergenerational justice with the non-identity, motivation and discounting problems; the environmentalism of the poor with its reframing of the whole clause; and the eco-authoritarian argument answered in four ascending replies ending in just transition.*

#### In plain words

Session 11 gave the policy formula for sustainability. This session gives what lies underneath it, because "sustainable development" is a formula and not a philosophy, and four prior questions decide how it is used.

**What has moral standing?** Three answers are available and they genuinely differ. The anthropocentric answer counts human beings; nature is protected because humans need it, enjoy it or will inherit it. The biocentric answer counts every individual living thing, each having a good of its own. The ecocentric answer counts wholes — species, habitats, ecosystems. ⚠️ The fastest way to show these are not three degrees of the same concern is the wilderness question: would it be wrong to destroy a wilderness that no human will ever see, use or know of? The anthropocentric answer must be no; the other two say yes, for different reasons.

⚠️ A practical warning follows, and it is worth heeding. Strong ecocentrism, pressed consistently, licenses coercive limits on human beings for the sake of systemic goods — which is the eco-authoritarian risk in Block F — and it sits badly with the capability framework the rest of this owner relies on. The position to adopt in a development answer is a **strong, non-instrumental anthropocentrism supplemented by duties of stewardship**, stated as a position and defended, rather than a slogan about harmony with nature.

**Is permanent growth coherent?** Naess's 1973 distinction sets up the question: shallow ecology fights pollution and depletion in the interest of affluent human welfare, deep ecology asks about the framework that generates the symptoms and affirms intrinsic value in non-human life. The difference is one of **depth of questioning**, not degree of concern. The degrowth argument then makes the point structurally: efficiency gains have been absorbed by expanding output, and beyond a threshold further output adds little to health, longevity or education in affluent societies. ⚠️ Its indispensable qualification is the asymmetry — **contraction and convergence**: reduction where consumption is far above any defensible threshold, expansion where it is far below. An answer that prescribes degrowth uniformly for a society below the deprivation threshold has misunderstood the argument, and saying so explicitly is what marks a controlled answer.

**What is owed to the unborn?** Duties of justice rather than benevolence — but three problems have to be met rather than ignored. The **non-identity problem** observes that different policies produce different people, so a person born into a degraded world cannot easily claim to have been made worse off by the policy that degraded it. The **motivation** problem observes that future people cannot reciprocate. The **discounting** problem observes that standard appraisal makes catastrophic distant harm look trivial, and the philosophical objection is that a **pure time preference** is arbitrary in the same way that discounting someone for living further away is arbitrary. The specification that makes the duty determinate is to owe **preserved options and avoided irreversibility** rather than particular goods.

**Who bears the cost of adjustment?** This is where the environmentalism of the poor reframes everything. If a community's subsistence depends directly on a forest or a river, then for that community environment and development are not competing objectives — they are the same question. ⚠️ "Environment versus development" is therefore a framing available only to people whose subsistence is not at stake, and the real question is distributive: who gains, who bears the risk, who decides. And when the eco-authoritarian argues that democracy is too slow for ecological survival, four replies answer in ascending strength, ending with the decisive one: an adjustment whose burden falls on those least able to bear it will be neither just nor durable, and a **just transition** conditions ecological restructuring on prior guarantees of livelihood, retraining, compensation and participation.

#### The doctrine, exactly

✅ **The three orientations.** *Anthropocentric*: human beings only; nature has instrumental value as resource, life-support, amenity and heritage for future humans; it protects only what humans happen to need or value and permits destruction where no human interest is engaged. *Biocentric*: all individual living beings, each having a good of its own; living things have inherent worth independent of use; it gives no principled way to adjudicate between individual organisms and cannot easily protect a species or a system as such. *Ecocentric*: ecological wholes — species, habitats, ecosystems, the biotic community; integrity, stability and resilience have value beyond the parts; it risks subordinating individuals, human and non-human, to the good of the whole.

⚠️ **The discriminating question.** Would it be wrong to destroy a wilderness that no human being will ever see, use or know of? The anthropocentric answer must be no; the biocentric and ecocentric answers are yes, for different reasons.

⚠️ **The practical warning.** ❌ Do not adopt strong ecocentrism casually. The defensible position for a development answer is a **strong, non-instrumental anthropocentrism supplemented by duties of stewardship**, stated and defended as a position.

✅ **Naess (1973).** *Shallow ecology* fights pollution and resource depletion, with the central objective being the health and affluence of people in the developed countries. *Deep ecology* asks about the underlying assumptions instead of the symptoms; it affirms the intrinsic value of non-human life independent of usefulness, treats richness and diversity of life forms as values in themselves, and calls for a change in the ideological framework — in the human self-understanding that places humanity outside and above nature. ⚠️ The difference is one of **depth of questioning, not degree of concern**.

⚠️ **The deep-ecology reconstruction.** (1) Symptom-directed policy leaves the generating framework intact. (2) That framework treats nature as a standing reserve and rising consumption as the measure of a good life. (3) While it is intact, efficiency gains are absorbed by expanded consumption and problems reappear at a larger scale. (4) An adequate response therefore revises the framework. (5) Hence the difference is depth of questioning.

⚠️ **Deep ecology's two objections and replies.** *It is a demand for conversion, not a policy, and risks equating the consumption of the poor with that of the affluent* — the strongest version distinguishes **subsistence from luxury** consumption so that restraint falls on affluence, but this substantially domesticates deep ecology and concedes ground; say so rather than concealing it. *Intrinsic value cannot be established without a valuing subject* — the reply is that the objection confuses the **source** of a value judgment with its **object**, though the residual problem is that intrinsic value remains asserted rather than demonstrated.

✅ **Productivism.** The assumption, shared across otherwise opposed ideologies, that expanding production is the master objective of social organisation — both the measure of success and the solvent of distributive conflict. ⚠️ The critique applies to **industrial socialism as much as to capitalism**, and saying so prevents the answer collapsing into a generic anti-market answer.

⚠️ **The degrowth argument.** (1) Economic activity is embedded in a biophysical system with finite sinks and stocks. (2) Efficiency improvements per unit of output have historically been offset by expansion in volume, so relative decoupling has not delivered absolute reduction at the required scale. (3) Beyond a threshold, further aggregate output adds little to health, longevity, education or reported well-being in affluent societies. (4) Therefore permanent aggregate growth is neither ecologically feasible nor developmentally necessary for already-affluent economies. (5) Hence the objective is a planned reduction in throughput, with provisioning reorganised around sufficiency, care, shorter working time and equality. (6) And the burden of reduction falls on high-consuming economies and classes, **not** on societies below the deprivation threshold.

⚠️ **Its weakest link, to be conceded.** That a non-growing economy can maintain employment, public services and social stability. ❓ No managed, socially stable contraction at scale has been demonstrated, and the concession should be made explicitly.

✅ **The asymmetry.** **Contraction and convergence** — reduction where consumption is far above any defensible threshold, expansion where it is far below. ❌ An answer that applies degrowth uniformly to a society below the threshold has misunderstood the argument.

⚠️ **The capability bridge.** If development is expansion of capability rather than of output, then beyond the level at which output secures capability, further growth has no automatic developmental claim. Sen's framework therefore reaches the same conclusion by a **non-ecological** route, and joining the two is a strong analytical move.

✅ **Intergenerational justice.** Present generations owe duties of **justice**, not merely of benevolence, to those not yet born — most plausibly a duty not to foreclose their capacity to meet their own needs and to live lives they have reason to value. The **Brundtland Report (1987)** builds this into the concept of sustainable development itself. ❌ Paraphrase that formulation; never reproduce it verbatim.

⚠️ **The three problems.** *Non-identity*: different policies produce different future people, so a person whose existence depends on the degrading policy cannot easily claim to have been made worse off by it; the standard response shifts to **impersonal** principles about the quality of lives lived, whoever lives them, or grounds the duty in the value of the resource base. *Motivation and reciprocity*: future people cannot reciprocate, vote, bargain or sanction, so mutual-advantage contractarianism struggles to generate the duty at all. *Discounting*: a **pure time preference** — counting a person's interests for less merely because she exists later — is arbitrary in the way that counting them for less because she lives further away is arbitrary.

⚠️ **The specification that answers indeterminacy.** The duty is best specified as **preserving options and avoiding irreversibility** — protecting the capacity to choose rather than the content of the choice. Uncertainty is therefore an argument **for precaution** about irreversible losses, not for inaction.

✅ **The environmentalism of the poor.** Associated with **Guha and Martinez-Alier, *Varieties of Environmentalism* (1997)**: environmentalism in the global South is typically not a post-material concern of the affluent for wilderness and amenity but a struggle over **livelihood** — access to forests, water, grazing, fisheries and clean air by communities whose subsistence depends directly on them.

⚠️ **Its five-step argument and the reframing.** A dominant account treats environmental concern as emerging only after material needs are satisfied; but sustained conflicts occur among the poorest over resources on which they directly depend; those struggles are simultaneously distributional and ecological; therefore environment and development are not opposed objectives for such communities; hence "environment versus development" is a framing available only to those whose subsistence is not at stake. ⚠️ The consequence is that the real question is **distributive** — who gains, who bears the risk, who decides, and on what terms.

✅ **Its objection and reply.** *It romanticises poor communities as natural conservationists, when poverty also drives degradation.* The thesis is about the **structure of conflict**, not ecological virtue: it claims that the poor fight over resources they depend on, which is compatible with degradation under insecure tenure. ⚠️ The strongest version argues that **secure rights convert dependence into stewardship**.

⚠️ **The eco-authoritarian argument.** (1) Ecological limits are hard constraints and breaching them is catastrophic and partly irreversible. (2) Democratic processes are slow, short-horizoned and vulnerable to organised interests and to voters who bear costs now for benefits later. (3) Therefore ecological survival may require insulating decisions from democratic contestation.

⚠️ **The four replies, in ascending strength.** *Epistemic*: ecological policy depends on dispersed local knowledge of soils, water, seasons and species that centralised authority does not possess and cannot easily acquire. *Compliance*: measures imposed without consent are evaded, reversed at the next opportunity, and require enforcement capacity that itself consumes resources. *Record*: insulation from accountability has not historically produced ecological restraint, and suppressing information and dissent removes the very feedback that detects damage early. *Distributive — the decisive one*: where the burden of adjustment falls on those least able to bear it, ecological policy will be neither just nor durable, and a **just transition** conditions ecological restructuring on prior guarantees of livelihood, retraining, compensation and participation.

⚠️ **The verdict.** The ecological case **strengthens rather than weakens** the argument for participation, because durable ecological transformation requires the consent and knowledge of those whose livelihoods it reorganises.

#### Named evidence you can cite

- **Arne Naess, the shallow/deep ecology distinction (1973)** ✅ — cited by position and year only; paraphrased, never quoted. The examinable formulation is *depth of questioning, not degree of concern*.
- **The Brundtland Report, *Our Common Future* (1987)** ✅ — duties to future persons built into the concept of sustainable development. ❌ Paraphrase only.
- **Guha and Martinez-Alier, *Varieties of Environmentalism* (1997)** ✅ — the environmentalism-of-the-poor thesis; the single strongest reframing of "development versus environment" and essential on tribal stems.
- **The three orientations on moral standing** ✅ — anthropocentric, biocentric, ecocentric, with the wilderness question that proves they are distinct positions.
- **The non-identity problem, pure time preference and the options-and-irreversibility specification** ⚠️ — the apparatus that makes a duty to the future arguable rather than merely asserted.
- **The just-transition reply** ⚠️ — the distributive answer to eco-authoritarianism and the constructive close of any environment stem.

#### ANSWER-GRABBING LINE — WRITE OR ADAPT IN THE EXAM

> The ecological limit binds affluent consumption far more tightly than it binds subsistence: a uniform prescription of restraint mistakes an argument about the distribution of ecological burdens for an argument against material improvement as such — and for a community whose subsistence depends on a forest or a river, environment and development are not competing objectives but the same question.

#### MUST-WRITE KEYWORDS

**moral standing** · **anthropocentric, biocentric, ecocentric** · **shallow and deep ecology** · **depth of questioning, not degree of concern** · **intrinsic value** · **productivism** · **throughput and sufficiency** · **contraction and convergence** · **subsistence against luxury consumption** · **intergenerational justice** · **non-identity problem** · **pure time preference** · **preserve options, avoid irreversibility** · **environmentalism of the poor** · **ecological distribution conflict** · **eco-authoritarianism** · **just transition**

#### HOW TO WRITE THE PARAGRAPH — claim → named evidence → analysis → qualification

> **Claim.** "Sustainable development" is a policy formula rather than a philosophy, and the prior questions it leaves unanswered — what has moral standing, whether permanent growth is coherent, what is owed to those not yet born, and who bears the cost of adjustment — determine how any development-and-environment stem should be answered. **Named evidence.** The anthropocentric, biocentric and ecocentric orientations differ over what counts morally rather than over how much nature to protect, as the wilderness question shows; Naess's distinction of 1973 separates a shallow ecology directed at pollution and depletion in the interest of affluent human welfare from a deep ecology that questions the framework generating those symptoms, the difference being one of depth of questioning rather than degree of concern; the productivism critique identifies the assumption, shared by industrial socialism as much as by capitalism, that expanding production is the master objective; the Brundtland Report of 1987 builds duties to future persons into the concept of sustainable development itself; and Guha and Martinez-Alier, in *Varieties of Environmentalism*, show that environmental conflict in the global South is typically a struggle over livelihood rather than a post-material concern for wilderness. **Analysis.** Taken together these convert the standard "environment versus development" stem into a distributive question — who gains, who bears the risk, who decides — and they defeat the eco-authoritarian argument on four ascending grounds, of which the decisive one is that an adjustment whose burden falls on those least able to bear it will be neither just nor durable, so a just transition conditions ecological restructuring on prior guarantees of livelihood, retraining, compensation and participation. **Qualification.** Two qualifications are compulsory: the degrowth argument applies asymmetrically as contraction and convergence, since prescribing it uniformly for a society below the deprivation threshold misreads it, and its weakest link — that a non-growing economy can maintain employment, services and stability — is undemonstrated; and a duty to future persons must meet the non-identity problem rather than assert itself, which is why it is best specified as a duty to preserve options and avoid irreversibility rather than to deliver particular goods.

#### Objection → reply → residual limit

- **Objection.** ✅ Precaution about irreversibility is a licence for indefinite obstruction. Almost every significant development decision forecloses something permanently, so a presumption against irreversible loss can be used to block any project whatever, including projects that would relieve severe deprivation.
- **Reply.** ⚠️ The presumption is **defeasible and graded**, not absolute: it attaches most strongly where the loss is large in scale, non-substitutable, and forecloses options for people who cannot participate in the decision — future generations, or a community excluded from it — and it weakens where the loss is small, substitutable or reversible. It also shifts the **burden of justification** rather than prohibiting action, requiring the proposer to show why the foreclosed option is not needed rather than requiring objectors to prove that it is.
- **Residual limit.** ❓ Grading the presumption requires judgments about substitutability and scale that are themselves contested, and the burden-shift can be discharged by an institution with the resources to produce assessments while a community affected by the decision cannot contest them. The philosophical argument fixes the direction of the presumption; it does not equalise the capacity to argue within it, which is exactly why participation and just transition belong in the same session.

#### Examiner traps on this session

1. Invoking "harmony with nature" as though it were an argument. Name the orientation, defend it, and accept its characteristic weakness.
2. Adopting strong ecocentrism casually. It licenses coercion for systemic goods and conflicts with the capability framework the rest of the answer depends on.
3. Prescribing degrowth uniformly. ❌ The argument is asymmetric — contraction and convergence — and applying it to a society below the deprivation threshold misreads it.
4. Asserting duties to future generations without meeting the non-identity problem. The duty must be argued, and the options-and-irreversibility specification is what makes it determinate.
5. Quoting the Brundtland formulation. ❌ Paraphrase only.
6. Naming an environmental datum, target, project, company, movement or region. ❌ Prohibited here; environmental facts and policy belong to the Environment and Governance areas.

#### Mini recap

Four prior questions. Standing: anthropocentric, biocentric, ecocentric — distinct positions, proved by the wilderness question; adopt a defended stewardship anthropocentrism rather than a slogan. Naess 1973: shallow against deep, a difference in depth of questioning. Productivism is shared by industrial socialism and capitalism; degrowth argues from finite sinks, absorbed efficiency and the post-threshold flattening — asymmetric as contraction and convergence, weakest at social stability. Intergenerational justice faces non-identity, motivation and discounting; specify it as preserving options and avoiding irreversibility. Guha and Martinez-Alier (1997) reframe "environment versus development" as distributive. Eco-authoritarianism is answered epistemically, by compliance, by record and decisively by distribution — just transition. Verdict: ecology strengthens the case for participation.

```text
  CLOSURE FLOW -- SESSION 19
  sustainability formula -> FOUR PRIOR QUESTIONS: standing | coherence of
      permanent growth | duties to the unborn | who bears adjustment
      -> MECHANISM: standing orientations -> Naess depth-of-questioning ->
         productivism/degrowth -> intergenerational duty -> environmentalism
         of the poor -> just transition
      -> CONSEQUENCE: "environment versus development" becomes DISTRIBUTIVE;
         ecology STRENGTHENS the case for participation
      -> TRAP: harmony slogans; casual ecocentrism; uniform degrowth;
         unargued duties to the future; quoting Brundtland; naming any
         project, region or figure
      -> ANSWER USE: name the orientation, run the asymmetry, specify the
         future duty as options-and-irreversibility, close on just transition
```

### SESSION 20 — DEVELOPMENT AND TRIBAL VALUES: THE FOUR-TEST RECONCILIATION AND THE INTEGRATED ANSWER SPINE

#### The picture first

```text
   PART A -- WHAT THE CONFLICT ACTUALLY IS
   =======================================
   NOT "MODERNITY VERSUS TRADITION". THAT FRAMING DECIDES NOTHING.
   THE CONFLICT IS FIVE QUESTIONS ABOUT AUTHORITY AND DISTRIBUTION:

     1. WHO DEFINES BENEFIT?
     2. WHO BEARS COST AND RISK?
     3. IS LAND ONLY A COMMODITY, OR ALSO IDENTITY AND RELATION?
     4. WHOSE KNOWLEDGE COUNTS?
     5. ARE CONSENT AND SELF-GOVERNMENT REAL?

   WHAT PROJECTS DESCRIBED AS DEVELOPMENT CAN IMPOSE
     DISPLACEMENT | EXTRACTION | ECOLOGICAL LOSS | CULTURAL
     ASSIMILATION -- while the BENEFITS FLOW ELSEWHERE.

   THE DISCIPLINE ON DESCRIBING COMMUNITIES

   | GENERALISATION. WHAT MANY DEVELOPMENT CONFLICTS DO INVOLVE:        |

   | CUSTOMARY INSTITUTIONS | INTERGENERATIONAL CONTINUITY |            |

        where grave uncertainty exists
     VERDICT: development is reconcilable with tribal values ONLY WHEN
     COMMUNITIES ARE **AGENTS** OF DEVELOPMENT RATHER THAN OBSTACLES
     OR OBJECTS.

   PART D -- THE FOUR TESTS. THIS IS THE MARK-BEARING STRUCTURE.
   A development project affecting a community dependent on a common
   resource must satisfy ALL FOUR JOINTLY:
     (i)   CAPABILITIES GAINED **AND DESTROYED** are both counted,
           INCLUDING THE COMMUNITY'S OWN
     (ii)  those bearing the cost PARTICIPATE IN THE DECISION, not
           merely in the COMPENSATION
     (iii) IRREVERSIBLE ecological and cultural losses attract a

  | FOREST RIGHTS ACT, 2006        | ENACTED RIGHTS-RECOGNITION      |
  |                                | STATUTE recognising individual  |
  |                                | and COMMUNITY forest rights,    |
  |                                | including community rights over |
  |                                | management and protection       |
  +--------------------------------+---------------------------------+
  | ORISSA MINING CORPORATION v.   | SUPREME COURT JUDGMENT involving|
  | MINISTRY OF ENVIRONMENT &      | GRAM SABHA CONSIDERATION of     |
  | FOREST (2013)                  | community and religious claims  |
  +--------------------------------+---------------------------------+
  | ARTICLES 48A and 51A(g)        | NON-JUSTICIABLE constitutional  |
  |                                | provisions -- Directive         |
  |                                | Principle and Fundamental Duty  |
  |                                | on protection of environment,   |
  |                                | forests and wildlife            |
  +--------------------------------+---------------------------------+
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+
   | ENACTMENT IS NOT NOTIFICATION. NOTIFICATION IS NOT IMPLEMENTATION. |
   | IMPLEMENTATION IS NOT CAPABILITY EXPANSION. A JUDGMENT IS NOT      |
   | LEGISLATION. A NON-JUSTICIABLE PROVISION ESTABLISHES COMMITMENT    |
   | AND NOT OUTCOME. AND NONE OF THEM PROVES A PHILOSOPHICAL POSITION. |
   +-------------------------------------------------------------------+

   PART F -- THE FIVE INTER-SCHOOL DEBATES, ON ONE SCREEN
     GROWTH MODEL vs SEN ...... unit: output vs substantive opportunity |
       person: consumer/factor vs AGENT | inequality: distribute after
       growth vs CONVERSION AND FREEDOM CENTRAL | democracy: possibly
       instrumental vs INSTRUMENTAL AND CONSTITUTIVE
     SEN vs NUSSBAUM .......... evaluative space + public reasoning vs
       LIST + DIGNITY THRESHOLD; Sen avoids paternalistic closure,

       scale CENTRALISES POWER, MULTIPLIES WANTS, DEGRADES LABOUR OR

       village as INSTITUTIONALISED CASTE EXCLUSION -> local
```
